We observed: at high x (47183) steps ~595; at low x (1650) steps ~0.26. Since step ∝ x² if x is ν. Let me test: dν ∝ ν² → step ~ c·ν². At ν=47183, step≈595; at ν=1650, step≈0.26. Alternatively if x is wavelength λ and the grating has linear dispersion in λ, dλ constant. But here dλ varies hugely, so x is likely ν (wavenumber), where steps scale as ν².

[tool-774] exec_command <- ASCII render of full spectrum and zoomed regions ($ cd /app && python3 << 'EOF' import numpy as np x=[];y=[] with open('graphene.dat') as f: for line in f: p=line.strip().split('\t') x.append(float(p[0].replace(',','.'))) y.append(float(p[1].replace(',','.'))) x=np.array(x);y=np.array(y) def ascii_plot(xx, yy, title, width=100, height=30): lo, hi = xx.min(), xx.max() ylo, yhi = yy.min(), yy.max() print('==', title, '== x:[%.1f, %.1f] y:[%.1f, %.1f]' % (lo, hi, ylo, yhi)) if yhi - ylo == 0: return grid = [[' ']*width for _ in range(height)] # map each point into grid (x reversed for nicer view: low->high left to right) xs = (xx - lo)/(hi-lo)*(width-1) ys = (yy - ylo)/(yhi-ylo)*(height-1) for a,b in zip(xs,ys): i=int(round(a)); j=int(round(b)) j = height-1-j # flip so high intensity is up grid[j][i] = '#' for row in grid: print(''.join(row)) # full spectrum ascii_plot(x, y, 'full') # zoom region around laser tail / peaks 15500-18000 (i.e., shifts 1150-3600) m=(x>=15500)&(x<=19500) ascii_plot(x[m], y[m], 'laser + peaks 15500-19500') # G region x 17200-17800, y 17000-23000 m=(x>=17200)&(x<=18000) ascii_plot(x[m], y[m], 'region 17200-18000') # 2D region x 15800-17000 m=(x>=15800)&(x<=17000) ascii_plot(x[m], y[m], 'region 15800-17000') EOF)

The ASCII plot isn't very informative due to scaling. Let me think more systematically.

The task: "Fit the G and 2D Peak of the spectrum and return the x0, gamma, amplitude and offset of the peaks". A Lorentzian fit: y = offset + amplitude / (1 + ((x - x0)/gamma)^2), or a pseudo-Voigt. The parameters: x0 (peak position), gamma (half width), amplitude, offset (background). So we fit each peak with a Lorentzian (common for Raman) riding on a linear/constant background.

We need to identify where G and 2D are in this data. In graphene Raman with a 532nm laser:
- G ~ 1580 cm^-1
- 2D ~ 2680 cm^-1

Hmm, that's suspicious: step ∝ x² could also mean x is inverse-wavelength-like (i.e., already ν) with linear pixel steps in ν²... no wait.

If a grating disperses linearly in wavelength, then pixel → λ linear. If x is ν = 1/λ, then dν/dλ = -1/λ², so dν = dλ/λ² = ν² dλ. Thus step in ν ∝ ν². That matches: the x column is ν (cm^-1), and the underlying detector pixel axis is linear in wavelength. The detector range in wavelength: λ_min = 1/47183.55 cm = 0.21194 µm = 211.9 nm; λ_max = 1/1648.72 cm = 6.065 µm. That spans 212 nm to 6 µm — that's an absurd range for one detector. Unless this is an FT-Raman spectrometer (NIR FT, e.g., 1064 nm excitation) whose wavenumber range in "absolute" space is huge? Hmm.

Actually, wait: FT-Raman spectrometers record in absolute wavenumber cm^-1 tooakes, often 0 to 15000 cm^-1 for room temp. 212 nm to 6 µm doesn't fit.

If x is wavelength in nm, then a linear grating in *wavenumber* would give dλ ∝ λ². Same pattern: step ∝ x². So we can't distinguish from step scaling alone whether x is λ with dλ∝λ² or ν with dν∝ν². Both have step∝x².

The 2D peak of graphene is typically a doublet (2D1, 2D2) when excited with certain lasers, i.e., two components. In many single-layer graphene spectra at 2.33 eV, the 2D band consists of two overlapping peaks separated by ~50-100 cm^-1. Here we see structure: 16382 (y=17254), 16313 (y=17835), 16245 (y=18159), 16178 (y=18120), 16111 (y=17840)... This could be the 2D doublet! Or could be two different 2D components.

[tool-ea8] exec_command <- Generate PNG plots then convert to ASCII art ($ cd /app && python3 << 'EOF' import numpy as np import matplotlib matplotlib.use('Agg') import matplotlib.pyplot as plt from PIL import Image import io x=[];y=[] with open('graphene.dat') as f: for line in f: p=line.strip().split('\t') x.append(float(p[0].replace(',','.'))) y.append(float(p[1].replace(',','.'))) x=np.array(x);y=np.array(y) def plot_to_ascii(ax_fn, title, w=110, h=44, filename=None): fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(w/10, h/10), dpi=60) ax_fn(ax) ax.set_title(title, fontsize=10) buf = io.BytesIO() fig.savefig(buf, format='png') plt.close(fig) buf.seek(0) img = Image.open(buf).convert('L') # Resize to target chars img = img.resize((w, h)) px = np.array(img) # map: black=0 -> '#', white=255 -> ' ' chars = np.array([' ', '.', ':', '-', '=', '+', '*', '#', '%', '@']) idx = np.clip((px // 26), 0, 9) lines = [''.join(chars[row]) for row in idx] print('====', title, '====') print('\n'.join(lines)) # full spectrum with log y def full(ax): ax.plot(x, y, lw=0.6) ax.set_xlabel('x'); ax.set_ylabel('intensity') ax.invert_xaxis() plot_to_ascii(full, 'FULL spectrum (linear y)', 130, 50) def full_log(ax): ax.semilogy(x, y, lw=0.6, base=10) ax.set_xlabel('x'); ax.set_ylabel('intensity (log)') ax.invert_xaxis() plot_to_ascii(full_log, 'FULL spectrum (log y)', 130, 50) # zoom 15500-19500 def zoom1(ax): m=(x>=15500)&(x<=19500) ax.plot(x[m], y[m], 'o-', ms=3, lw=0.8) ax.set_xlabel('x') plot_to_ascii(zoom1, 'zoom 15500-19500', 140, 44) # zoom in shift space 1200-3200 def zoom2(ax): nu=19139.5435 m=(nu-x>=1200)&(nu-x<=3200) ax.plot(nu-x[m], y[m], 'o-', ms=4, lw=0.8) ax.set_xlabel('Raman shift from 19139.5') plot_to_ascii(zoom2, 'shift 1200-3200 around x=16245 peak', 150, 45) EOF)

Let me approach this computationally and carefully. Let me examine the structure more: the data set seems to consist of a spectrum with a huge feature at 19139, and probably the actual graphene peaks are also present but masked. Let me separate the spectrum into pieces around the laser. Actually, maybe the file is organized in "segments" — like a single spectrum but with "dead" pixels (gap) where the laser peak saturates the detector? The y intensity around the laser region is ~79000-80000 (max 79400) — high but not clipped.

Let me think about the actual graphene peaks relative to the laser at 19139.5:
- G at 1580 → x=17559.5
- 2D at 2680 → x=16459.5

Looking at data near x=17559: the values are 19755, 19327, 19004, 18560, 18199 — these continue the laser's declining tail好好的, with no visible bump. To detect the G peak on this steep tail, we would need to fit a Lorentzian on top of the tail.

Looking at data near x=16459: values: 16639 (x=16451), 17254 (x=16382), 17835 (x=16313), 18159 (x=16245), 18120 (x=16178), 17840 (x=16111)... There's clearly a big peak structure around x=16245 (with multiple bumps), which would be the 2D.

But wait, the 2D at shift 2680 should be at x=16459, NOT 16245. The structure between 16451 and 16111 could be a 2D doublet centered around 16245 (shift ~2894). Hmm.

Could the laser actually be at a different wavenumber? Let me measure the exact peak position. The five points around the top: 
- 19429.90 → 78849
- 19332.11 → 79083
- 19235.33 → 78815
- 19139.54 → 79400
- 19044.74 → 78843
So max at 19139.54 but points at 19332, 19235, 19044 are almost as high (~78800-79000). This looks like a broad, saturated/clipped peak (flat tops). The "peak" at 19139.54 could be just a random fluctuation on top of a saturated plateau. It's likely the Rayleigh line is CLIPPED (saturated) — a plateau from 18950 to 19430.

This suggests the Rayleigh line is the saturated one)Skip the laser at ~19185 maybe? Hmm.

OK let me take yet another angle. What if this output file is from an instrument that reports "Raman shift in cm^-1" but the calibration is such that the Rayleigh line is at 0 and the G peak at ~1580... but the data shows a huge peak at 19139 which is impossible. Unless... the x-axis is not Raman shift but the "detector position" axis in cm^-1 of the *spectrometer's* absolute wavenumber. So Raman shift = laser_cm^-1 - x. In that case G≈1580 → x=17559.

Given the data is dense near low x and sparse near high x, and the peak at 10289 (y=18805), 6329 (13777), 3745 (12920) — there are multiple peaks at low x. Could these low-x peaks be the same G/2D peaks detected in different grating ORDERS? Let me check: for a grating with multiple orders, the same wavelength appears at different diffraction orders... but the x-axis in wavenumber would then have replicated features. The peaks at 10289.94, 6329.37, 3745.05 might correspond to...? If the laser were at 19139.54, then the D peak at shift 1350 → x=17789; G → 17559; 2D → 16459. None match 10289, 6329, 3745.

Alternatively, maybe this Raman spectrum is actually of a sample excited with a laser at ~10289 cm^-1 (972 nm)?? then peaks... nah.

I think the most productive approach is to look at the data visually by rendering the region around the laser at full resolution as a line plot. Since I can't display inline, let me create an HTML file or use IPython? Actual approach: generate the plot and print the PNG as ANSI truecolor in the terminal using image-to-ANSI. Let me write a script that prints the plot using ANSI block characters with 256-color. That could be readable in the transcript.
[tool-d2a] exec_command <- Render zoomed plot as ANSI truecolor blocks ($ cd /app && python3 << 'EOF' import numpy as np import matplotlib matplotlib.use('Agg') import matplotlib.pyplot as plt from PIL import Image import io, sys x=[];y=[] with open('graphene.dat') as f: for line in f: p=line.strip().split('\t') x.append(float(p[0].replace(',','.'))) y.append(float(p[1].replace(',','.'))) x=np.array(x);y=np.array(y) def ansi_img(fig_fn, w=120, h=50): fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(w/3, h/3), dpi=100) # larger canvas fig_fn(ax) buf=io.BytesIO() fig.savefig(buf, format='png') plt.close(fig); buf.seek(0) img = Image.open(buf) # crop axes area not needed; just resize whole img = img.resize((w, h)) px = np.array(img.convert('RGB')) out=[] for row in px: line='' for r,g,b in row: line += f'\x1b[38;2;{r};{g};{b}m\u2588' out.append(line) return '\n'.join(out) def zoom_plot(ax): m=(x>=15500)&(x<=19600) ax.plot(x[m], y[m], '.-', lw=年报 none) # mark peaks ax.set_title('zoom 15500-19600') # Just output a discrete colored "line chart" using console chars is hard. # Instead print intensity as colored blocks over 2D grid of (x, log(y)). print("Use matplotlib save & convert; saving zoom PNG.") fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(16,5), dpi=100) m=(x>=15500)&(x<=19600) ax.plot(x[m], y[m], '.-', ms=5, lw=0.8) ax.set_xlabel('x (abs wavenumber)') ax.set_ylabel('intensity') ax.set_title('zoom 15500-19600') plt.tight_layout(); plt.savefig('zoom_peak.png') print('saved') EOF echo done)

[tool-54a] exec_command <- ASCII plot of dense region log-scale ($ cd /app && python3 << 'EOF' import numpy as np x=[];y=[] with open('graphene.dat') as f: for line in f: p=line.strip().split('\t') x.append(float(p[0].replace(',','.'))) y.append(float(p[1].replace(',','.'))) x=np.array(x);y=np.array(y) def render(xx, yy, xlo, xhi, ylo, yhi, width, height, logy=False): m=(xx>=xlo)&(xx<=xhi) xx=xx[m]; yy=yy[m] grid=[[' ']*width for _ in range(height)] if logy: def trafo(v): return np.log10(v) a, b = trafo(ylo), trafo(yhi) ys=(trafo(yy)-a)/(b-a)*(height-1) else: ys=(yy-ylo)/(yhi-ylo)*(height-1) xs=(xx-xlo)/(xhi-xlo)*(width-1) for a,b in zip(xs,ys): i=int(round(a)); j=height-1-int(round(b)) if 0<=i<width and 0<=j<height: grid[j][i]='#' for row in grid: print(''.join(row)) print('x:[%.1f,%.1f] y:[%.1f,%.1f] log=%s'%(xlo,xhi,ylo,yhi,logy)) # entire dense region 1600-19500, log y render(x, y, 1600, 19500, 20, 80000, 180, 50, logy=True) # region 10000-19500 render(x, y, 10000, 19500, 20, 80000, 180, 50, logy=True) # region 15800-18200 linear render(x, y, 15800, 18200, 15500, 25000, 140, 40, logy=False) EOF)

[tool-430] exec_command <- Fit Lorentzian to dominant peak at 16245 ($ cd /app && python3 << 'EOF' import numpy as np from scipy.optimize import curve_fit x=[];y=[] with open('graphene.dat') as f: for line in f: p=line.strip().split('\t') x.append(float(p[0].replace(',','.'))) y.append(float(p[1].replace(',','.'))) x=np.array(x);y=np.array(y) def lor(x, x0, g, A, off): return off + A*g**2/((x-x0)**2 + g**2) # Fit the 2D-like peak at 16245 using region 15600-16800 m=(x>=15600)&(x<=16800) xx=x[m]; yy=y[m] # initial guess x0g=16245; gg=300; Ag=2000; off=16500 popt, pcov = curve_fit(lor, xx, yy, p0=[x0g,gg,Ag,off], maxfev=20000) perr = np.sqrt(np.diag(pcov)) print('2D-peak fit region 15600-16800:') print('x0=%.3f gamma=%.3f A=%.3f off=%.3f'%tuple(popt)) res = yy-lor(xx,*popt) print('rmse=%.2f maxres=%.2f'%(np.sqrt(np.mean(res**2)), np.max(np.abs(res)))) # Fit with an asymmetric background to account for the tail rising with x: y=off + m*x + lor def lor_bg(x, x0, g, A, off, m): return off + m*x + A*g**2/((x-x0)**2+g**2) popt2,_=curve_fit(lor_bg, xx, yy, p0=[16245,300,1600,30000,-0.8], maxfev=20000) print('with linear bg:', popt2) # Region 16700-18100? Check if anything fits a G-like Lorentzian m2=(x>=16800)&(x<=18200) xx2=x[m2]; yy2=y[m2] # This is the laser tail; fit a decaying model + possible gaussian peak def tail_lor(x, x0, g, A, off, a, b): return off + a*np.exp(b*x) + A*g**2/((x-x0)**2+g**2) try: popt3,_=curve_fit(tail_lor, xx2, yy2, p0=[17560,50,200,1500,1e8,-4e-4], maxfev=30000, bounds=([17000,0,-5000,-inf,-inf,-inf],[18200,200,5000,30000,inf,0])) print('G-region tail+lor fit:', popt3) except Exception as e: print('G fit failed:', e) EOF)

[tool-095] exec_command <- Joint fit: laser tail + G + 2D Lorentzians ($ cd /app && python3 << 'EOF' import numpy as np from scipy.optimize import curve_fit x=[];y=[] with open('graphene.dat') as f: for line in f: p=line.strip().split('\t') x.append(float(p[0].replace(',','.'))) y.append(float(p[1].replace(',','.'))) x=np.array(x);y=np.array(y) def rest(x, a, b, c, x0g, gg, Ag, x0d, gd, Ad): return a*np.exp(b*x)+c + Ag*gg**2/((x-x0g)**2+gg**2) + Ad*gd**2/((x-x0d)**2+gd**2) m=(x>=16000)&(x<=18150) xx=x[m]; yy=y[m] # To help convergence, fit tail using tails first then refine globally # tail region considered (16600..18150) minus the 2D region # We'll do a global fit with bounds p0=[1e9, -4e-4, 14000, 17560, 40, 400, 16207, 184, 2200] try: popt, pcov = curve_fit(rest, xx, yy, p0=p0, maxfev=50000) perr = np.sqrt(np.diag(pcov)) print('joint fit (tail+G+2D):') names=['a','b','c','x0_G','gamma_G','A_G','x0_2D','gamma_2D','A_2D'] for n,p,e in zip(names,popt,perr): print(f'{n:6s} = {p:12.4f} ± {e:.3f}') res=yy-rest(xx,*popt) print('rmse=%.2f maxres=%.2f'%(np.sqrt(np.mean(res**2)), np.max(np.abs(res)))) except Exception as e: print('failed', e) EOF)

[tool-fea] exec_command <- Constrained joint fit G and 2D on tail ($ cd /app && python3 << 'EOF' import numpy as np from scipy.optimize import curve_fit x=[];y=[] with open('graphene.dat') as f: for line in f: p=line.strip().split('\t') x.append(float(p[0].replace(',','.'))) y.append(float(p[1].replace(',','.'))) x=np.array(x);y=np.array(y) # Background: combination of a constant + linear (local background) plus two Lorentzians def model(x, offset, slope, x0g, gg, Ag, x0d, gd, Ad): bg = offset + slope*(x-16500) return bg + Ag*gg**2/((x-x0g)**2+gg**2) + Ad*gd**2/((x-x0d)**2+gd**2) m=(x>=15800)&(x<=18200) xx=x[m]; yy=y[m] p0=[16500, 0.0, 17560, 60, 400, 16210, 200, 2300] bounds=([10000, -5, 16500, 1, 0, 15800, 20, 0], [25000, 5, 18000, 400, 5000, 16500, 700, 10000]) try: popt, pcov = curve_fit(model, xx, yy, p0=p0, bounds=bounds, maxfev=50000) perr=np.sqrt(np.diag(pcov)) names=['offset','slope','x0_G','gamma_G','A_G','x0_2D','gamma_2D','A_2D'] print('constrained joint fit 15800-18200:') for n,p,e in zip(names,popt,perr): print('%-7s %10.4f ± %8.2f'%(n,p,e)) res=yy-model(xx,*popt) print('rmse=%.2f maxres=%.2f'%(np.sqrt(np.mean(res**2)),np.max(np.abs(res)))) except Exception as e: print('fit failed', e) EOF)

OK here's yet another idea: maybe the x-axis is in "Wavelength (nm)" and the two peaks (G and 2D) are at 3745 nm and 6329 nm... no.

Let me try to figure out the axis by looking at the step: at x=47183 step is 595, at x=1650 step is 0.26. If x is wavelength in nm with calibration x = A/(ν_pixel)... For a linear-in-wavenumber grating, pixel ∝ ν, so x=λ=1/ν would have step ∝ 1/pixel², i.e., dλ ∝ λ²? Let me test step vs λ² for x as λ: corr(step, x²) = 0.9999 (we computed with x being the column). So if x is λ, step ∝ λ² — this means the GRATING is linear in wavenumber (constant step in ν → dλ = (dλ/dν)dν = -λ²dν∝λ²). That's a nonstandard grating but possible (e.g., a "constant-wavenumber" grating is unusual).

If the grating is linear in PIXEL→ν (constant step in ν), then the x-axis would be ν with EQUAL spacing — but we see unequal spacing. So the x-axis is NOT ν with constant step. If the grating is linear in PIXEL→λ (common), then x-axis = 1/λ (ν) with step ∝ ν², OR x-axis=λ with... wait:

Case A: pixel ∝ λ (linear grating). Then λ = a·pixel + b. If x = ν = 1/λ, then dν/dfpixel = -1/λ² · dλ/dpixel ∝ ν². We see step ∝ x². ✓ So x = ν (wavenumber) on a linear-in-λ grating.

Case B: pixel ∝ ν (constant wavenumber per pixel, nonstandard). Then ν = a·pixel+b, x=ν would have CONSTANT step. We see variable step, so x≠ν.
If x=λ on pixel∝ν grating, dλ/dfpixel ∝ -λ². ✓ again step∝x². So x could be λ on such nonstandard grating.

Both cases fit the step∝x² observation because dλ/dν ∝ -λ² and dν/dλ ∝ -ν² are reciprocal. We need another clue.

The laser at x=19139.54: if x=ν (cm^-1), laser ≈522.5 nm. If x=λ (nm), laser at 19139.54 nm = 19.14 µm — deep IR, unusable for typical Raman of graphene, PLUS the range 47183 nm (=47 µm) to 1648 nm (1.65 µm) covers FIR-to-NIR — a single detector spanning 1.65-47 µm is absurd.

So x is ν (cm^-1) — ABSOLUTE photon wavenumber. Laser at 19139.5 cm^-1 (522.5 nm). Confirmed.

Given all this, G and 2D (graphene) should be at x ≈ 17559.5 (shift 1580) and x ≈ 16459.5 (shift 2680). Even if G isn't visually obvious, that's where physics places it Dropped.

Given the problem statement is short and this is likely a test/task, the data was probably GENERATED for the purpose, with clear G and 2D peaks for a graphene Raman spectrum. Let me think about what a generated "Raman spectrum of graphene" looks like on an ABSOLUTE wavenumber axis:
- Laser/Rayleigh at ~19139 (huge saturated peak) — actually a real setup's Rayleigh.
- G peak at 17559 (1580 shift) — a small peak.

OK let me take yet another approach: let me compute the "Raman shift" of the peak at 16245.58 for DIFFERENT assumed laser positions and see which gives a "nice" number:
- laser 19139.54 → shift 2893.96
- laser 18925 (to make 16245.58 → 2680): laser = 18925.58... 
- The 2D peak of graphene at 532nm excitation is ~2680. For 16245.58 to be 2D at 2680, laser=18925.58 cm^-1 (528.4 nm). For the G at 1580, x_G = 18925.58-1580 = 17345.58.
- Is there a feature at x=17345.58? Data at 17333.73 (y=18560.41), 17411.69 (19004.15). Hmm no peak, just descending tail.
- If the laser is 19139.54, G at x=17559, which has no feature either.

So G is invisible either way — because it's hidden in the tail. Actually, wait: in a real Raman spectrum, the G peak is usually VISIBLE as a bump even if the laser tail is present, because the laser tail can be very steep but G at 1580 shift is quite far from the laser (1580 cm^-1 away), so tail already weak by then. In our data, the tail drops from 79000 (laser) to ~18000 at x=17256; so at G position (17559, shift 1580) the tail is already ~19000. A G peak with amplitude ~500-1000 would be visible as a bump. There's no bump. So G is either absent (sample is bilayer/defective?) or very weak.

Actually, in RAMAN of graphene, the G peak intensity is typically comparable to the 2D peak (ratio I2D/IG ≈ 2-7 for monolayer). If 2D ≈ 18000 at its peak, the "intensity above baseline" of 2D is ~1500-2500cars. So G's amplitude should be a few hundred to ~1000. On the declining laser tail with local noise ±100 and slope ~-60/step... a G of amplitude 500 with width 30 (spanning ~2-3 data points spacing ~78) would create a visible plateau/maximum. None seen. So G is really not there.
